FOREWORD 

Megt. c) la ber

This Directory of Labor Organizations in Europe, issued in two volumes, is one of a series of directories of trade unions throughout the world, published by the Office of International Labor Affairs of the U. S. Department of Labor. 

The preceding publications include the Directory of International 

Trade Union Organizations, in three parts:

The International Confederation of Free Trade Unions (ICFTU); 

The International Trade Secretariats (ITS); and The International Federation of Christian Trade Unions (CISC). 

They also include the Directory of the Communist World Federation of 

Trade Unions (WFTU). 

A directory of the Western Hemisphere Labor Organizations will be issued shortly; directories of labor organizations in Asia and Australasia, and in Africa are in preparation. 

The present Directory contains the best available information on the structure, composition and international affiliation of national trade union federations and their affiliates in Europe. With few exceptions, changes which have occurred since December 31, 1955, have not been incorporated in this Directory. 

In order to present a complete picture of organizations in the labor field in each country, the Directory also includes labor organizations which, because of their relationship to their governments, do not have the typical characteristics and functions common to the free trade unions.

To facilitate the addition of supplemental information or the replacement of obsolete information, whether it is a whole chapter or individual pages, the Directory is designed in loose-leaf form and divided into chapters. Altogether there are thirty country chapters and one chapter, No. 31, relating to the International Center of Free 

Trade Unionists in Exile. 

From time to time, as the need arises, supplementary or replacement chapters or pages will be prepared. 

Acknowledgement and thanks are given to the many individuals and organizations who have cooperated and assisted in the preparation of the Directory.

DIRECTORY OF LABOR ORGANIZATIONS IN EUROPE Chapter 1 ALBANIA

CHAPTER 

ALBANIA 

The population of Albania, as of December 31, 1955, was 

1,406,000. About 50% of these, or 700,000, were in the labor force. Due to the non-industrial character of the country the number of wage and salary earners (no data are available) is apparently small since the trade union organization, in spite of existing economic pressure to join the unions, reports only 90,000 members, which represent 13% of the labor force. 

As in all other Communist-controlled countries, the trade union organization is an arm of the government, and its primary purpose is implementation of government-party policy with regard to the working population. The list of affiliates of the Union of Albanian Trade Unions (Bashkimet Profesionale te Shqiperise BPSh) includes a number of professional associations, which apparently have some official ties with the central trade union organization.

DIRECTORY OF LABOR ORGANIZATIONS IN EUROPE Chapter 2 AUSTRIA

CHAPTER - AUSTRIA 

The population of Austria was about 6,975,000 in 1955. 

Of that number 3,420,000, or 50%, were in the labor force.

About two-thirds (2,139,000) of the labor force were wage and salary earners. Over half of the wage and salary earners were members of trade unions, virtually all affiliated with the Austrian Federation of Trade Unions AFTU (Österreich ischer Gewerkschaftsbund - UGB). 

The OGB comprises elements from the various political parties. By internal agreement, UGB offices were allocated to the Socialists, People's Party and, initially, also to the Communists before the unsuccessful Communist uprising in 1951. 

The strongest group within the UGB is Socialist oriented. 

About three-fourths of the votes in workers' council elections are for the Socialist ticket while less than 15% are for 

Communist candidates.

The OGB, though a member of the International Confederation of Free Trade Unions (ICFTU), has permitted its "People's Party" faction which claims the adherence of about 90,000 members to join the International Federation of Christian 

Trade Unions (Confédération Internationale des Syndicats 

Chrétiens CISC) and has agreed to pay the corresponding affiliation fees.

DIRECTORY OF LABOR ORGANIZATIONS IN EUROPE Chapter 3 BELGIUM

HONUAL MI CHOTTARIUÆUTO POSAL 40 YROTUDTAR

CHAPTER 

BELGIUM 

About 41%, or 3,481,027, of the last reported population, 8,512,195 (census 1947) were in the labor force. More than 

70% of these, or 2,486,111, were counted as wage and salary earners. Nearly 60% of them were members of trade unions. 

Practically all trade union federations are politically oriented. Of the two major federations, the Belgian General 

Federation of Labor (Fédération Generale du Travail de 

Belgiqué - FGTB) and the Confederation of Christian Trade 

Unions (Confédération des Syndicats Chrétiens de Belgiqué CSC), the former has close links to the Socialist Party while the equally strong CSC maintains a relationship with the Catholic Party (Christian-Democrat Movement). 

The General Federation of Liberal Unions of Belgium 

(Centrale Générale des Syndicats Libéraux de Belgiqué CGSLB), the trade union arm of the Liberal Party, is a member of the World Union of Free Trade Unions (WUFTU), a rather unimportant international confederation of Liberal unions in a few European countries with its seat in Ghent. 

The weak Communist United Trade Union, an affiliate of the World Federation of Trade Unions (WFTU), has ceased to exist.

DIRECTORY OF LABOR ORGANIZATIONS IN EUROPE Chapter 4 BULGARIA

DIMECTOSA G2 FVRON OKOVIH SALON ZA PWKARAD

CHAPTER 

BULGARIA 

The population of Bulgaria as of December 31, 1955, was 

7,619,000. Slightly over one-half, or 4,000,000, were believed to comprise the labor force. At the end of 1953 wage and salary earners totaled 948,000, of which more than 97% or 920,000 were trade union members. This apparently reflects certain economic pressures exerted upon the workers to join the unions.

As in all other Communist-controlled countries the trade union organization is an arm of the government, and its primary purpose is implementation of government-party policy with regard to the working population. The list of affiliates of the Bulgarian Central Council of Trade Unions (Tsentralniya suvet na profesionalnite suyuzi TSSPS) includes a number of professional associations which may have some official ties with the central trade union organization. Names of trade union officials appearing in this chapter are not necessarily the officials' surnames. Since individuals in Bulgaria may be known either under their surname or under their middle name, which is the given name of the father, frequency of usage was the criterion for selecting the name listed.

DIRECTORY OF LABOR ORGANIZATIONS IN EUROPE Chapter 5 CYPRUS

ORO ACEAS NO POTCARTO

CHAPTER - CYPRUS 

In accordance with the Colonial Report on Cyprus for 1954 Cyprus had a population of 513,700. The gainfully employed numbered 260,000 or about 50% of the population. 

There were 87,000 wage and salary earners outside of agriculture, fishing, forestry and domestic service. Trade union membership totals about 24,500 or about 28% of the above wage and salary earners. 

The majority of the trade union members, about 80%, are organized in the Pancyprian Federation of Labor, "Old 

Trade Unions" (Pankypria Ergatiki Omospondia - PEO) with 

Communist-oriented leadership. The International Confederation of Free Trade Unions (ICFTU) affiliate, the Cyprus

Workers' Federation, "New Trade Unions" (Synomospondia Ergaton Kyprou SEK) has less than 15% of the union membership. Both unions have strong Greek orientation.

Another small trade union represents Cypriots of Turkish nationality.

DIRECTORY OF LABOR ORGANIZATIONS IN EUROPE Chapter 6 CZECHOSLOVAKIA

CHAPTER 

CZECHOSLOVAKIA 

At the end of 1955 the population of Czechoslovakia was 13,065,000, of which 6,000,000, or less than 50%, comprised the labor force. No information is available on the number of wage and salary earners. In 1955 it was claimed that 

3,500,000 were trade union members. 

Within the Czechoslovakian central trade union organization special consideration is given to the Slovakian areas of Czechoslovakia for which a distinct regional organization is established.

As in all other Communist-controlled countries, the trade union organization is an arm of the government and its primary purpose is implementation of government-party policy with regard to the working population.

DIRECTORY OF LABOR ORGANIZATIONS IN EUROPE Chapter 7 DENMARK

CHAPTER DENMARK 

Roughly, half of the population of 4,383,000 are in the labor force. Of the 2,118,000 which comprise the labor force there are 1,497,729, or about 70%, reported to be wage and salary earners. About 46% of these, some 700,000, are organized in the Federation of Danish Trade Unions - FDTU (De samvirkende Fagforbund i Danmark), a member of the 

International Confederation of Free Trade Unions (ICFTU). 

There are only splinter groups outside of this Federation.

DIRECTORY OF LABOR ORGANIZATIONS IN EUROPE Chapter 8 FINLAND

CHAPTER 

D 

FINLAND 

Finland had a population of 4,029,803 in 1950 which had grown to 4,165,300 in 1953. The last available data on the labor force are for 1953 when it comprised 1,984,282, or over 47% of the population. About 58% of the labor force in 1950, or 1,152,710, were wage and salary earners. Somewhat less than one-third of the wage and salary earners were organized at that time.

Of the 331,958 members of trade unions reported in 1955 more than two-thirds were members of the Confederation of 

Finnish Trade Unions (Suomen Ammattiyhdistysten Keskusliitto 

SAK). The Confederation at present is without international affiliation although many of its affiliates have joined the anti-Communist International Trade Secretariats (ITS). 

A large number of white collar workers are organized in a Confederation of Intellectural Workers (Henkisen Työn Keskusliitto - HTK), which has no international ties but is known for its non-political orientation. 

Some of the unions outside of these confederations may be members of the Communist World Federation of Trade Unions' (WFTU) Trade Union Internationals, which are also known as 

Trade Departments. 

---

Ni to robiszobotreb

DIRECTORY OF LABOR ORGANIZATIONS IN EUROPE Chapter 9 FRANCE

CHAPTER - FRANCE 

The population of France according to the 1946 census, the latest count available, is 39,819,838, of which 20,520,466, or about 50%, comprise the labor force. Almost two-thirds of the labor force, or 13,391,770, are wage and salary earners. 

There is no reliable information available on the number of active trade union members. While claimed membership ranges as high as 5,500,000, which is not more than 40% of the wage and salary earners, estimates go even as low as 1,500,000 or around 10% of the wage and salary earners. 

The strongest trade union federation, the Communist 

General Confederation of Labor (Confédération Général du 

Travail - CGT), an affiliate of the Communist World Federation of Trade Unions (WFTU), had a membership of more than 6,000,000 after World War II. It has continuously lost members since the exodus of the non-Communist groups which formed the General Confederation of Labor - Workers' Force 

(Confédération Generale du Travail Fource Ouvrière in 1947. It is believed that of the estimated 5,000,000 members lost since 1947 the great majority have remained unorganized although many of them may still support the CGT 

in Social Security elections. 

CGT-FO)

The CGT-FO is a member of the International Confeder ation of Free Trade Unions (ICFTU). The French Confederation of Christian Workers (Confédération Francaise des Travailleurs Chretiens CFTC), which has maintained its independence, is 

a member of the International Federation of Christian Trade unions (Confédération Internationale des Syndicats Chrétiens CISC). An independent federation, the General Confederation of 

Supervisory Employees (Confédération Générale des Cadres - CGC), shows considerable strength.

Most recently there have been tendencies attempting to carve nation-wide industrial or professional federations out of the affiliates of the various politically-oriented confederations.

Publication: 

Remarks: 

1/ See Remarks: 

Syndicalisme Indépendant 

Formed 1948. In September 1952, a split occurred and two separate confederations operated under the same name, the majority group under the leadership of Sulpice Dewez, and the minority group under the leadership of André Parsal. In October 1955, the 

Parsal group set up a new organization under the name of Confédération Syndicale 

Travail et Liberté (CSTL). (See p. 9.49) 

Before the split the joint membership was estimated to be 80,000.

The CGSI is strongest in the metallurgical industry (Renault Plants at Billancourt, Flins, Le Mans; Simca at Nanterre and 

Poissy), the chemical industry (Rhône 

Poulenc), and the petroleum industry (Shell- Berre). 12-31-55

DIRECTORY OF LABOR ORGANIZATIONS IN EUROPE Chapter 10 GERMANY (WEST) (Federal Republic of Germany and Western Berlin)

day Aderonu gelyes)

DILECLSHA. (4. IN BONS OFURUTSVARČNÚ DIY FOROLT

CHAPTER 10 - GERMANY (WEST) (Federal Republic of Germany and Western Berlin) 

In 1955 the population of West Germany was 50,162,000, of which 24,984,000, or nearly 50%, were in the labor force. 

The number of wage and salary earners was 18,302,000, or almost three-fourths of the labor force. More than 35% of the wage and salary earners, or about 6,500,000, are members of trade unions. 

The German Trade Union Federation for the area of the Federal Republic and Berlin (Deutscher Gewerkschaftsbund fuer das Gebiet der Bundesrepublik Deutschland und Berlin DGB) is the strongest trade union federation with more than 6,000,000 members, or about 93% of the total of organized wage and salary earners. The DGB is the successor to the separate politically-oriented trade union federations which existed before the Hitler era and World War II. One-third of the white collar workers have formed their own independent federation with more than 400,000 members while most of the other white collar workers belong to DGB affiliates.

Outside of these two confederations there are a number of splinter groups, none of them with a great following.

Most recently a dissident group of Christian trade unions have formed a Christian trade union federation which up to now has not been able to gather any significant strength. 

The DGB is a member of the International Confederation of Free Trade Unions (ICFTU) while the German Salaried Employees' Union (Deutsche Angestellten Gewerkschaft DAG), the white collar workers' federation, has applied for ICFTU membership with the request still pending.

Three of the small splinter groups are members of the 

International Federation of Christian Trade Unions (Confédér ation Internationale des Syndicats Chrétiens - CISC).

DIRECTORY OF LABOR ORGANIZATIONS IN EUROPE Chapter 11 GERMANY (EAST) 

(GDR 

German Democratic Republic)

CHAPTER 11 - GERMANY (EAST) 

(GDR 

German Democratic Republic) 

The population of East Germany (including the Soviet Zone of Berlin) was estimated at 17,900,000 as of December 31, 1955.

The total number of wage and salary earners was 6,100,000 at the end of 1953. Latest figures for trade union membership indicate that 5,500,000, or over 90% of the wage and salary earners, are organized, which may reflect economic pressures exerted upon the workers to join the unions. 

As in all other Communist-controlled countries, the trade union organization is an arm of the government, and its primary purpose is implementation of government-party policy with regard to the working population.

B. Robaju

DIRECTORY OF LABOR ORGANIZATIONS IN EUROPE Chapter 12 GREAT BRITAIN

CHEVEL DITWEA 

CHEDPE TS 

DIMEGLOKA OD TYEOH ONGEMISVILOJE IN ZAKOVER

CHAPTER 12 

GREAT BRITAIN The 

In 1955 the population of Great Britain was 50,768,000, of which 23,875,000, or 47%, were in the labor force. number of wage and salary earners in 1951 was 20,499,900, or about 86% of the labor force.

The British Trades Union Congress (TUC), an affiliate of the International Confederation of Free Trade Unions (ICFTU), reported a membership of 8,101,798 in 1954. Among its 187 affiliates are both industrial and craft unions. The overwhelming majority of organized labor belong to the TUC. 

The General Secretary is the chief full-time executive officer of the confederation. The Chairman of the General 

Council, which is the executive organ empowered to carry out the policies decided by the annual Congress, customarily succeeds to the office on the basis of seniority as members of the Council.

The Scottish Trade Union Congress (STUC), which is referred to in the list, is an associated body of trade unions in Scotland, most of them affiliated directly or indirectly with the British TUC.

DIRECTORY OF LABOR ORGANIZATIONS IN EUROPE Chapter 13 GREECE

CHAPTER 13 - GREECE 

The population of Greece in 1953 was 7,831,295 and probably about 8,000,000 in 1955. The labor force was estimated in 1955 to have comprised less than half of the population, or about 3,800,000. No data are available on wage and salary earners.

More than four-fifths, or 370,000, of the total trade union membership of about 455,000 belong to the Greek

General Confederation of Labor (Geniki Synomospondia Ergaton 

Hellados GSEE).

Structually, the GSEE, a member of the International Confederation of Free Trade Unions (ICFTU), is organized on both an industrial trade union and an area basis. The area offices are called Labor Centers. 

Government employees have their own organization, the Supreme Council of Civil Servants (ADEDY), which comprises nearly 15% of the total trade union membership.

000,000,o qhtorodnem volna

DIRECTORY OF LABOR ORGANIZATIONS IN EUROPE Chapter 14 HUNGARY

CHAPTER 

<D 

HUNGARY 

The population of Hungary as of December 31, 1955, was estimated at 9,369,000, of which 4,500,000, or about 48%, were in the labor force. There is no information available on the number of wage and salary earners, but, due to the comparatively small industrialization of the country, they probably do not exceed 50% of the labor force, or 2,250,000. 

Trade union membership reportedly totaled 1,913,000, or about 90% of the assumed number of wage and salary earners. 

The high percentage may reflect economic pressures exerted upon the workers to join unions. 

As in all other Communist-controlled countries, the trade union organization is an arm of the government, and its primary purpose is implementation of government-party policy with regard to the working population. The list of affiliates of the Hungarian National Trade Union Council (Szakszervezetek Orszagos Tanacsa SZOT) includes a number of professional associations and non-worker groups, active in the private sector of the economy, which apparently have some official ties with the central trade union organization. 

Information which has become available on the leadership of Budapest branches of SZOT affiliates is included in the list.

DIRECTORY OF LABOR ORGANIZATIONS IN EUROPE Chapter 15 ICELAND

DIBWMCKA CE IVANU OHGVRILELIQUE IN 2010B7

CHAPTER 15 

ICELAND 

The population of Iceland in 1950 was 144,300 of which 64,237, or about 45%, were in the labor force. As of 1946 the number of wage and salary earners was 41,943. About 30,000, or nearly 70%, of the wage and salary earners are members of trade unions. 

The vast majority of its organized workers are concentrated in the Icelandic Federation of Labor - IFL (Althydusamband 

Islands - ASI), which reports a membership of 27,500. The

Federation is a member of the International Confederation of Free Trade Unions (ICFTU). Although a slate of left-wing 

Socialists and Communists were able to gain control of the Federation at the 1955 convention there has been no change in the status of affiliation to the ICFTU.

DIRECTORY OF LABOR ORGANIZATIONS IN EUROPE Chapter 16 IRELAND

CHAPTER 16 

IRELAND The 

In 1951 the population of Ireland was 2,960,593, of which 1,272,038, or about 43%, were in the labor force. number of wage and salary earners was 712,765 which represented about 56% of the labor force. The latest data reveal that there are 407,000 members of unions. Union members are almost evenly divided between the Irish Trade Union Congress ITUC (Codhail na gCeard- 

Chumann), closely linked to the British trade union movement, and the Congress of Irish Unions CIU (Comar Ceard Eireann), which is an independent Irish trade union organization. 

Recently, there have been agreements between the two federations for closer cooperation with a view to final merger. 

The ITUC, through its relations to the British TUC, is indirectly linked to the international anti-Communist trade union movement. The CIU, however, has no international relations whatsoever, except that some of its affiliates are members of the anti-Communist International Trade Secretariats (ITS). A move, some years ago, to join the International 

Confederation of Free Trade Unions (ICFTU), was rejected by the Congress.

DIRECTORY OF LABOR ORGANIZATIONS IN EUROPE Chapter 17 

(including TRIESTE)

CHAPTER 17 ITALY 

(including TRIESTE) 

In 1954 Italy, without Trieste, had a population of 48,399,000, of which 21,342,000, or about 43%, were in the labor force. The number of wage and salary earners was 10,721,000 or about 50% of the labor force.

Trieste reported a population of 292,925 in 1953 and a labor force of 125,725. The number of wage and salary earners in 1952 totaled 89,052.

In reporting trade union membership for Italy (excluding Trieste) only claimed figures have been used because of the difficulty of arriving at reasonable estimates. Based on claimed figures, the number of trade union members may be as high as 8,000,000, or almost three-fourths of the wage and salary earners. On the basis of conservative estimates the total number of organized workers is probably closer to 5,000,000.

The strongest confederation is the Communist Italian General Confederation of Labor (Confederazione Generale Italiana del Lavoro 

CGIL), an affiliate of the World Federation of Trade Unions (WFTU).

The anti-Communist Italian Confederation of Labor Unions (Confederazione Italiana Sindacati del Lavoratori - CISL), which was established in 1950 as an outgrowth of a secession in 1949 of non-Communist trade unions from the formerly unified CGIL, is the second strongest confederation, by far the strongest anti-Communist organization, and still growing. CISL is non-political. Its members and officers come from all political non-Communist shades, mostly Demo-Christians, but also including members of Partito Socialista Democratico Italiano (PSDI) and Partito Repubblicano Italiano (PRI). The other anti-Communist confederation, the Italian Union of Labor (Unione Italiana del Lavoro UIL), has a predominant PSDI and PRI membership. Both CISL and UIL are members of the International Confederation of Free Trade Unions (ICFTU). Of the other national centers only the Italian Confederation 

National Workers' Unions (Confederazione Italiana Sindacati Nazionali Lavoratori CISNAL), often referred to as a neo-fascist group, has some importance. 

In Trieste the trade union membership totals about 48,000 with almost two-thirds in the anti-Communist Confederal Chamber of Labor (Camera Confederale del Lavoro CCdL), an ICFTU affiliate, and one-third in the Communist Confederation of Labor (Confederazione del Lavoro - CONFLAVORO).
